Choreographic Quick Changes: First-Class Location (Set) Polymorphism CCS: Theory of computation Functional constructs CCS: Theory of computation Type structures CCS: Computing methodologies Concurrent programming languages
Ashley Samuelson, Andrew K. Hirsch, Ethan Cecchetti

Abstract
. Choreographic programming is a promising new paradigm for programming concurrent systems where a developer writes a single centralized program that compiles to individual programs for each node. Existing choreographic languages, however, lack critical features integral to modern systems, like the ability of one node to dynamically compute who should perform a computation and send that decision to others. This work addresses this gap with λ \lambda qc , the first typed choreographic language with first class process names and polymorphism over both types and (sets of) locations. λ \lambda qc also improves expressive power over previous work by supporting algebraic and recursive data types as well as multiply-located values. We formalize and mechanically verify our results in Rocq, including the standard choreographic guarantee of deadlock freedom.



stix@largesymbols"0E
stix@largesymbols"0F

1.
Introduction

Concurrent programs are integral to many modern software systems, but programming them correctly is notoriously difficult.
Traditionally, each process in the system runs a separate program, but developers must reason about the interactions between these programs and the order in which these events occur.
This complex behavior can easily lead to bugs such as deadlocks, where execution stalls due to nodes with mismatched send and receive expectations forever waiting on each other.

Choreographic programming

(
Montesi 2023
;
Montesi 2013
)
is an emerging paradigm that promises to simplify development of correct concurrent systems.
A choreography is a single top-level program that describes the computation performed by every node, including the interactions between them.
A compiler then
projects
programs for individual nodes from this single top-level program.
Choreographies centralize code, putting global control flow in one place and leading to
deadlock-freedom by design
,
structurally eliminating a notoriously challenging issue in concurrent code.

The theory of choreographies has advanced rapidly in the last several years
with the addition of higher-order functions
(
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2022
;
Hirsch and Garg 2022
)
,
polymorphism over types and processes
(
Graversen et al. 2024
)
, and multiply-located values
(
Bates et al. 2025
)
.
However, none of these results allow for
dynamic computation and sending
of process names in a
type-safe manner
.
The ability to treat host names as first-class values and share them between nodes is critical to many practical applications, such as dynamic load balancers.
The only existing work supporting first-class host names
(
Sweet et al. 2023
)
entirely lacks a type system,
and consequently lacks critical type-safety properties.

This paper presents the choreographic Quick Change calculus,
\lambda
qc
, the first choreographic language
to support first-class process names and types.
To understand the value of these features, and choreographic programming in general,
consider a simple cloud computing example where a client
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
wishes to outsource expensive computation
F
on input
X
.
If
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
wishes to run
F
on a specific (statically known) worker
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}W}
, they can do so using the following choreography.
Here
t@\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
indicates a value of type
t
located at
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{runAtW}:(t\to t^{\prime})@\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\to t@\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\to t^{\prime}@\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}

\displaystyle\mathsf{runAtW}~F~X=\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}W}.f\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}F\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}W}\\
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}W}.x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}W}\\
\end{array}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}W}.(f~x)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\end{array}

The notation
F\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}W}
means that whoever owns
F
(in this case
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
) should send it to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}W}
.
So in this program,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
sends the code (
F
) and the input (
X
) to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}W}
, who locally binds them to variables
f
and
x
, respectively.
Then
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}W}
computes
f~x
and sends the result back to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
.

Real systems, however, typically include a pool of workers and a load balancer to manage task assignment.
Clients contact this pool manager, who then (a) selects a worker, (b) notifies the worker of their new client, and (c) sends the client the worker’s identity.
To implement such a thread pool in a choreography, the client
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
can ask a pool manager
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}
where to run the task,
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}
must be able to reply with a dynamically chosen identity.
A choreography for such a process might look as follows,
where
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}acquireWorker}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}releaseWorker}
are local operations by the pool manager
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}
to locally track the state of the thread pool and select and return workers to the pool, respectively.

\displaystyle\mathsf{runWithWorker}:(t\to t^{\prime})@\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\to t@\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\to t^{\prime}@\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}

\displaystyle\mathsf{runWithWorker}~F~X=\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}W\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}acquireWorker}()\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}pool}\\
W.f\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}F\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}W\\
W.x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}W\\
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}.\mathit{res}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}W.(f~x)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\end{array}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~W.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}``done"}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1};}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}.(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}releaseWorker}~w)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1};}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}.\mathit{res}\end{array}

On the first line of this program, the pool manager
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}
selects an idle worker from the pool
and sends this
dynamically computed
value to the client
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
, where it is bound to the variable
W
.
Additionally, workers in the
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}pool}
are all notified about which worker was chosen to prevent situations where a worker is not aware that they have been selected.

As in the earlier
\mathsf{runAtW}
function, the client then sends
F
and
X
to
W
,
who binds them to
f
and
x
, computes
f~x
locally, and sends the result to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
.
Finally, the selected worker notifies the manager that they have finished the job, the manager releases the worker back into the thread pool, and the computation finishes by yielding the result.

The first line of this program has very similar syntax to sending and receiving local data, but is critically different:
it sends a location name which is then bound to the variable
W
known to all parties in
\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}pool}

and allows the programmer to use it as a location name within its scope.
This is the core feature of
\lambda
qc
: enabling
first-class location names
which can be dynamically computed (and hence quickly changed) at runtime.
While this feature appears simple on its face, it hides several important complexities.
First, the value is known to a set of hosts, not just a single host, making it multiply-located.
Second, location names in choreographies are generally part of the language of
types
, not values.
To support first-class location names without the need for dependent types,
we carefully separate locations from other values in the
\lambda
qc
type system, while still allowing interaction between the two.
These key insights allow us to intermingle dynamically chosen host names with process polymorphism, such as in the example above.

The main contributions of this work are as follows.

•

We generalize the constraints on the local (message) language of
Pirouette

(
Hirsch and Garg 2022
)
to allow for polymorphism, vastly increasing the expressivity of local computations (Section
3
).

•

We present
\lambda
qc
, the first typed choreographic programming language with the ability to send and receive first-class location names and types as messages.
Our language includes polymorphism over types, processes, and sets of processes, algebraic and recursive data types, and multiply-located values.
We formulate a sound, System F-like type system for our language without relying on dependent types or an operational semantics for types (Section
4
).

•

We define a network language (Section
5
) which serves as the compilation target for our endpoint projection procedure.
We show that compilation is complete, and is sound when all executed local computations are terminating.
This result, along with the soundness of our type system, allows us to prove that projected systems are always deadlock-free (Section
6
).

•

We formalize and verify all results in the Rocq Prover (formerly Coq).
This is the first mechanized formalization both of process (set) polymorphism and of multiply-located values (Section
7
).

2.
Background

To understand the contributions of this work, it is helpful to understand some background on choreographies with higher-order functions, process polymorphism, and multiply-located values.

2.1.
Pirouette

We generalize and build on Pirouette
(
Hirsch and Garg 2022
)
, a higher-order choreographic programming language.
As with other choreographies, Pirouette uses a located syntax inspired by the “Alice and Bob” syntax of cryptographic protocols.
To specify that
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
should (locally) compute
2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3
and send the result to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
, one would write
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
.
For clarity, we use
\mathsf{sans\text{-}serif}
for these source-level operations and color
choreographic operations in
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}blue}}
,
local operations in
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}green}
,
and location constants in
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}red}
.

One core aspect of Pirouette’s design that we inherit is a clear separation between choreographic operations and local operations.
The choreographic operations are fixed by Pirouette, and are agnostic to the local language.
The local operations can be specified in nearly any language whose values—possibly including functions—can be communicated between nodes via message-passing.
The local language need only have an operational semantics, a type system, and a type that can act as a boolean
(every value of that type can be used as
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}true}
or
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}false}
).

To connect the choreographic and local languages, Pirouette offers a form of let-expression that binds local variables to the output of a choreography.
For instance, the result of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
is a choreographic value located at
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
,
so using it in
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
’s local computation requires binding a
local
variable with the output of a
choreographic
computation.
The following program demonstrates how
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
can perform this binding and use the result locally as the variable
x
.

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B})}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.(4\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}*}}x)}

Pirouette also supports conditionals
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}then}}~{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}else}}~{C_{2}}
where the choreography itself branches based on the result of
C
, which should yield a boolean at some location
\ell
.
However, only
\ell
can access the branch condition,
so others with differing behavior in the
L
(eft) and
R
(ight)
branches cannot proceed without knowing which to take.
The location
\ell
can communicate the choice
d\in\{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}},\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}\}
of branch to another location
\ell^{\prime}
using a
selection statement

{\ell}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}[}{d}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\ell^{\prime}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1};}}C
.

At the choreographic level, Pirouette is simply typed, containing only base types and function types.
The base types are of the form
t@\ell
, and describe a value of local type
t
—any type from the local language’s type system—located at
\ell
.
This mixing of the local and choreographic type systems and the local variables bound by let-expressions, shown above, forces the type system to separately track both choreographic and local variables.

Pirouette defines two separate semantics:
one directly at the choreographic level to allow developers to more easily reason about the behavior of the choreography,
and one using a translation called
endpoint projection
(EPP) that defines how to compile a choreography to separate programs for each location.
Despite operating directly on a choreography, the first semantics also captures concurrent interleavings
by allowing out-of-order execution as long as it does not reorder the operations for any individual location.
For example, consider the program shown below.

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1};}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}.(5\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}*}}4)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}

The local computations for
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
involve disjoint parties, so they can execute in either order.
The sends, however, both involve
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
and must occur in the order specified:

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
must receive 5 from
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
before receiving 20 from
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
.

EPP defines how to compile a choreography into separate programs for each location that include only the operations that location needs to run.
The location being projected to is denoted by a subscript to the projection operator, as in
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}
and
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}
.
For instance, sending a value from
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
requires
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
to compute the value and send it, and requires
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
to receive from
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
:

\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}=\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}send}}~{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3})}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}to}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}=\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}recv}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}from}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}

We write operations of our target (network) language in
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}orange~teletype}}
font.

Hirsch and Garg 2022
prove that running these projected programs in parallel produces the same result as the first semantics,
meaning that developers can safely reason using the top-level semantics and, critically, any projected choreography is deadlock-free,
meaning it cannot get stuck with locations waiting to receive messages from each other (or for any other reason).

2.2.
Process Polymorphism

While this work is the first to support
first-class
locations in a typed choreography,
PolyChor
\lambda

(
Graversen et al. 2024
)
introduced the notion of process polymorphism,
providing a way to abstract over the participants in a choreography.
PolyChor
\lambda
introduces a
process abstraction

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Lambda}{\ell}\ldotp{C}
, akin to a classic type abstraction,
that binds variable
\ell
representing a process name in choreography
C
.
In our syntax, a programmer could write the following process function in which
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
computes a value and sends it to a yet to be determined recipient
\ell
.

F={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Lambda}{\ell}\ldotp{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\ell}

In PolyChor
\lambda
, the only way to use a process abstraction is to apply it to a location (e.g.,
F~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
or
F~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
)
or a location variable created by another process abstraction (e.g.,
F~\ell
).
Location names are not first-class values that can be computed locally or sent between parties.

Despite process names being statically resolvable in PolyChor
\lambda
, the projection of a process abstraction requires locations to behave differently depending on what the variable
\ell
resolves to
at run time
.
This is accomplished using an “AmI” construct in the compiled language that produces a local branch and reflects the fact that each process should know its own identity.
Specifically, the program
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{E_{1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{E_{2}}
will execute
E_{1}
when running at
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
and
E_{2}
when running elsewhere.
EPP can then project a process abstraction to an
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}
statement where each branch is a different projection of
C
:

\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}
replaces
\ell
with the current process, while
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}
assumes
\ell
has resolved to a different name.
That is,

\mathchoice{\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Lambda}{\ell}\ldotp{C}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Lambda}{\ell}\ldotp{C}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Lambda}{\ell}\ldotp{C}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Lambda}{\ell}\ldotp{C}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\Lambda}{\ell}\ldotp{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\ell\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\right]}}{C[\ell\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}]}{C[\ell\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}]}{C[\ell\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}]}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\ell\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\right]}}{C[\ell\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}]}{C[\ell\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}]}{C[\ell\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}]}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\ell\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\right]}}{C[\ell\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}]}{C[\ell\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}]}{C[\ell\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}]}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\ell\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\right]}}{C[\ell\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}]}{C[\ell\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}]}{C[\ell\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}]}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}}}.

A feature of many polymorphic languages which is notably missing from PolyChor
\lambda
is
recursive types
.
The addition of recursive types to this language would be challenging because their type system—based on System
F_{\omega}
—includes an operational semantics, and as a result their endpoint projection procedure requires types which appear in the choreography to be fully-reduced type values.
Implementing this feature would require either that endpoint projection handle
choreographies in which nonterminating types may appear, or that recursive
types are limited in order to force type computations to converge.
Both of these options have significant drawbacks.

2.3.
Multiply-Located Values

An alternative to the selection messages Pirouette and similar choreographies
use in choreographic conditionals is
multiply-located values

(
Bates et al. 2025
;
Sweet et al. 2023
)
.
These generalize the notion of local values at one location to local values at a set of locations.
This feature recognizes that, after sending a value, all parties involved know that value,
allowing the choreography to guarantee that the locations agree.

For instance, the choreography
\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}.(2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}<}}3)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.21529pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}

specifies that
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
should
both
compute
2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}<}}3
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
should send the result to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
.
Notably, the result of this computation is the multiply-located boolean value
\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}true}
,
meaning a condition could branch on the result without requiring further synchronization between these three parties.

3.
System Model

Before introducing the choreographic constructs in
\lambda
qc
, we specify the assumptions on the setting in which it operates.
As is standard in the choreography literature, we assume a fixed set of locations
\mathcal{L}
, each with a unique name.
Names are taken to be opaque identifiers associated with an underlying node, process, thread, etc.

Like Pirouette
(
Hirsch and Garg 2022
)
,
\lambda
qc
abstracts over the language for local computation, requiring only that it satisfy a small set of rules.
To support type and location polymorphism and multiply-located values,
\lambda
qc
adds a few assumptions beyond prior work,
but it still supports numerous languages.

The local language must have a syntax that specifies a set of values, a small-step operational semantics, and a type system.
We write
e_{1}\mathrel{\longrightarrow}e_{2}
to denote that a local term
e_{1}
steps to
e_{2}
in the local language’s semantics.
The semantics must satisfy two properties:

(1)

Values cannot step. That is, if
\operatorname{Val}({v})
, then there is no
e
such that
v\mathrel{\longrightarrow}e
.

(2)

Local steps satisfy the
diamond property
.
That is, if
e_{1}\mathrel{\longrightarrow}e_{2}
and
e_{1}\mathrel{\longrightarrow}e_{3}
,
then either
e_{2}=e_{3}
or there is some
e_{4}
such that
e_{2}\mathrel{\longrightarrow}e_{4}
and
e_{3}\mathrel{\longrightarrow}e_{4}
.

Property (
1
) is taken from Pirouette,
while property (
2
) ensures multiply-located computations all produce the same result.
Property (
2
) may appear restrictive, but many pure functional languages and all deterministic languages enjoy it.
We believe it can be weakened to the more general confluence statement that,
if
e_{1}\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}e_{2}
and
e_{1}\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}e_{3}
then
e_{2}\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}e_{4}
and
e_{3}\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}e_{4}
for some
e_{4}
,
but we leave the challenge of mechanically verifying this conjecture to future work.

3.1.
Local Kinding and Type System

As
\lambda
qc
is constructed generically over the local language,
\lambda
qc
’s type system—and the guarantees it provides—depend on the local language specifying a type system.
In particular, we require the local language to specify both a type system and a kinding system.
For simplicity, we show only a single local kind (
*_{e}
) here, but the results generalize to an arbitrary non-empty set of local kinds, which our Rocq formalization allows.
The local type system can be polymorphic, but this is not required—it may instead be a simple type system.

\frac{\alpha\in\Gamma}{\Gamma\Vdash\alpha}\frac{\Gamma\Vdash t_{1}\quad\Gamma,\alpha\Vdash t_{2}}{\Gamma\Vdash\mathchoice{t_{2}{\left[\alpha\mapsto t_{1}\right]}}{t_{2}[\alpha\mapsto t_{1}]}{t_{2}[\alpha\mapsto t_{1}]}{t_{2}[\alpha\mapsto t_{1}]}}\frac{\Gamma;\Sigma\Vdash e:t}{\Gamma\Vdash\Sigma}\frac{\Gamma;\Sigma\Vdash e:t}{\Gamma\Vdash t}\\
\frac{\Gamma\Vdash\Sigma\quad x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut\in\Sigma}{\Gamma;\Sigma\Vdash x:t}\frac{\Gamma;\Sigma\Vdash e_{1}:t_{1}\quad\Gamma;\Sigma,x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{1}\Vdash e_{2}:t_{2}}{\Gamma;\Sigma\Vdash\mathchoice{e_{2}{\left[x\mapsto e_{1}\right]}}{e_{2}[x\mapsto e_{1}]}{e_{2}[x\mapsto e_{1}]}{e_{2}[x\mapsto e_{1}]}:t_{2}}\frac{\Gamma\Vdash t_{1}\quad\Gamma,\alpha;\Sigma\Vdash e_{2}:t_{2}}{\Gamma;\Sigma\Vdash\mathchoice{e_{2}{\left[\alpha\mapsto t_{1}\right]}}{e_{2}[\alpha\mapsto t_{1}]}{e_{2}[\alpha\mapsto t_{1}]}{e_{2}[\alpha\mapsto t_{1}]}:\mathchoice{t_{2}{\left[\alpha\mapsto t_{1}\right]}}{t_{2}[\alpha\mapsto t_{1}]}{t_{2}[\alpha\mapsto t_{1}]}{t_{2}[\alpha\mapsto t_{1}]}}

Figure 1.
Required-admissible kinding and typing rules for the local language.

The local kinding and type systems are specified by a type well-formedness judgment
\Gamma\Vdash t
and an expression typing judgment
\Gamma;\Sigma\Vdash e:t
, respectively.
The double-vertical turnstile
\Vdash
indicates the local kind and type systems.
In each judgment
\Gamma
is a kinding context, and
\Sigma
is a typing context.
We only require that the rules in Figure
1
be admissible—they must be true of the system, but need not be axioms.
These rules are standard for polymorphic type systems.

As in Pirouette, the local type system must include a type, which we write as
\mathsf{bool}
,
where every value can be interpreted as either
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}true}
or
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}false}
.
These are required to define the behavior of conditionals, which branch on local values.
We also require three more types:

\mathsf{loc}_{\rho}
,
\mathsf{locset}_{\rho}
, and
\mathsf{tyRep}
,
defining the first-class local
representations
of location names, sets of locations, and local types, respectively.
The defining property of representations is that we can convert values of type
\mathsf{loc}_{\rho}
to location names in
\mathcal{L}
,
values of type
\mathsf{locset}_{\rho}
to
sets
of location names,
and values of type
\mathsf{tyRep}
to well-formed types in the local language.
For example, one could represent location names using integers, mapping 0 to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
, 1 to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
, and all other integers to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
.
We write representations of a type, location name, or set of location names as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}t{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}L{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}
, or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\{L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}
respectively, to disambiguate from the resolved type
t
, location
L
, or set of locations
\{L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}\}
.
In the above example,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}
is syntactic sugar for 0, while
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
refers to the
actual
location
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\in\mathcal{L}
.
A location need not have a unique representative, but each value can only represent one location.
There is also no requirement that all possible booleans, location names, etc. be present,
or even that the local types be inhabited, but uninhabited types will render choreographic features unavailable.

The subscript
\rho
in the type
\mathsf{loc}_{\rho}
is an upper bound on the set of locations to which an expression of that type may resolve.
For instance, both
{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}if}~{e}~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}then}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}}~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}else}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}}
can have type
\mathsf{loc}_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}
, but
{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}
cannot.
For
\mathsf{locset}_{\rho}
, the annotation must be a superset of any value of this type (e.g.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}:\mathsf{locset}_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}}
).
The precision of
\rho
does not explicitly affect the operational semantics of our language,
but an imprecise annotation may require a choreography to add unnecessary communication to remain well-typed (see Section
4.4
).
In practice, this static upper bound could be computed in a multitude of ways, such as refinement types
(
Freeman and Pfenning 1991
)
or a separate static analysis.
In this work, we assume for simplicity that the annotations are given directly by the local type system, and leave computing them precisely to future work.

Lastly, the local type system must provide standard progress and preservation guarantees:

•

Local Progress:
If
{}\Vdash e:t
then either
e
is a value, or there is some
e^{\prime}
such that
e\mathrel{\longrightarrow}e^{\prime}
.

•

Local Preservation:
If
\Gamma;\Sigma\Vdash e:t
and
e\mathrel{\longrightarrow}e^{\prime}
, then
\Gamma;\Sigma\Vdash e^{\prime}:t
.

3.1.1.
Example Local Languages

Many simply-typed and polymorphic
\lambda
-calculi satisfy our requirements with very minor modification.
To show that our requirements are both reasonable and satisfiable we present two examples here, with the full details contained in our formalization.

Example 1 (Simply-Typed
\lambda
-Calculus).

The simply-typed, call-by-value
\lambda
-calculus with primitive integers satisfies our requirements.
Specifically, integers can represent both booleans (zero is
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}false}
while all non-zero values are
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}true}
) and location names.
Having many representations of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}true}
is not a concern, as each representation remains unambiguous.
The space of both location sets and types,
\mathsf{locset}_{\rho}
and
\mathsf{tyRep}
, respectively, can both be the empty type,
as not all representations are required to exist.
This choice will render first-class location sets and local types unavailable at the choreographic level,
but does not otherwise interfere with the availability of choreographic type-, location-, or location-set–polymorphism.

Example 2 (System F).

System F with primitive booleans, integers representing locations, and lists of integers representing location sets satisfies our requirements.
Like using integers for
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}true}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}false}
, having multiple ordered lists represent the same set of locations is not a concern.
We can also include recursive functions, as there is no requirement that local expressions terminate.

To represent a non-trivial set of local types, we can define
\mathsf{tyRep}
to be a primitive data type with constructors
{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathsf{int}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathsf{bool}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}
, and
e_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathord{\to}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}}}e_{2}
.
Since the representations are expressions not types,
\mathsf{tyRep}
behaves identically to the other primitive types of the language.
The syntax of the resulting example language is shown below.

\begin{array}[]{@{}llcl@{}}\textrm{Types}&t&\Coloneqq&\alpha\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{loc}_{\rho}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{locset}_{\rho}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{int}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{bool}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{list}({t})\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{tyRep}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mut_{1}\to t_{2}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\forall\alpha.t\\
\textrm{Expressions}&e&\Coloneqq&x\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathop{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}fun}}{f}({x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\coloneqq}}{e}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mue_{1}~e_{2}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\Lambda}\alpha.e\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mue~t\\
&&\mkern 3.0mu\mid&n\in\mathbb{Z}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mue_{1}\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}e_{2}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mue_{1}\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}=}}e_{2}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mue_{1}\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}<}}e_{2}\\
&&\mkern 3.0mu\mid&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}true}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}false}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}if}~{e}~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}then}~{e_{1}}~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}else}~{e_{2}}\\
&&\mkern 3.0mu\mid&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}nil}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}cons}({e_{1}},{e_{2}})\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}case}~{e}~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}of}~({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}nil}}\Rightarrow{e_{1}})~({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}cons}({x},{y})}\Rightarrow{e_{2}})\\
&&\mkern 3.0mu\mid&{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathsf{int}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathsf{bool}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mue_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathord{\to}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}}}e_{2}\end{array}

As an example of how the type system is used to provide a static upper-bound on representations of locations, two of the typing rules for the
\mathsf{loc}_{\rho}
type are given below.

\frac{n\in\rho\subseteq\mathbb{Z}\quad\Gamma\Vdash\Sigma}{\Gamma;\Sigma\Vdash n:\mathsf{loc}_{\rho}}\frac{\Gamma;\Sigma\Vdash e:\mathsf{bool}\quad\Gamma;\Sigma\Vdash e_{1}:\mathsf{loc}_{\rho}\quad\Gamma;\Sigma\Vdash e_{2}:\mathsf{loc}_{\rho}}{\Gamma;\Sigma\Vdash\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}if}~{e}~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}then}~{e_{1}}~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}else}~{e_{2}}:\mathsf{loc}_{\rho}}

4.
The
\lambda
qc
Language

We now present the Quick Change Choreographic calculus (
\lambda
qc
),
a polymorphic
\lambda
-calculus for choreographies
that supports communication of dynamically generated (sets of) location names and types,
as well as multiply-located values and algebraic and recursive data types,
while retaining the traditional guarantee of deadlock freedom.

4.1.
\lambda
qc
Syntax

\begin{array}[]{@{}llcl@{}}\textrm{Selection Labels}&d&\Coloneqq&\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}\par\\
\textrm{Choreographies}&C&\Coloneqq&X\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\rho.e\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathop{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}fun}}}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{C}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0muC_{1}~C_{2}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Lambda}{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\ldotp{C}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0muC~t\\
&&\mkern 3.0mu\mid&\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}fold}}~{C}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}unfold}}~{C}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu(C_{1},C_{2})\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}fst}}~{C}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}snd}}~{C}\\
&&\mkern 3.0mu\mid&\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}inl}}~{C}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}inr}}~{C}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}case}}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}of}}~(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}inl}}~{X}\Rightarrow{C_{1}})~(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}inl}}~{Y}\Rightarrow{C_{2}})\\
&&\mkern 3.0mu\mid&C\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.21529pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}}\rho\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu{\ell}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}[}{d}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1};}}C\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}then}}~{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}else}}~{C_{2}}\\
&&\mkern 3.0mu\mid&\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\rho.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{C_{2}}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\rho.\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{C_{2}}\\
\end{array}

Figure 2.
Syntax of Choreographies

Figure
2
presents the full syntax of
\lambda
qc
.
To visually differentiate classes of variables, we write choreographic program variables in uppercase Roman characters (
X,Y,F,\dotsc
),
local program variables in lowercase Roman characters (
x,y,f,\dotsc
),
and type, location, and location set variables in lowercase Greek characters (
\alpha,\beta,\dotsc
).
The metavariable
\ell
denotes a location,
\rho
a set of locations,
\tau
a choreographic type,
t
a local type, and
\kappa
a kind.

Much of the
\lambda
qc
syntax consists of standard algebraic and recursive datatypes lifted to choreographies, but there are a few forms of note.
First, the term
\rho.e
specifies that the set of locations
\rho
should run local program
e
.
Note that
\rho
must be non-empty, as an empty set of locations performing a computation would be meaningless.
We use the shorthand
\ell.e
to mean
\{\ell\}.e
.
Local programs can use local variables bound in the scope of either the choreography or the local program itself.
For these variables, every location has a separate namespace, so
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x
denotes variable
x
in the namespace of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
.
Each location’s namespace is separate, so that
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x\neq\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.x
, and this is reflected in the substitution and renaming operations.
Local variables can be multiply-located, where we write
\rho.x
to mean that the local variable
x

is in the namespace of every location in the set
\rho
.

A core feature of choreographies is message passing, which
\lambda
qc
denotes as
C\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.21529pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}}\rho

to indicate sending the result of evaluating
C
to all locations in the set
\rho
.
Here
C
must produce a local value located at
\ell
(potentially among others);
\ell
then sends that value as a message to everyone in
\rho
.
When the result of
C
exists only at a single location, we elide the
\ell
on the arrow for simplicity.
After the message send, the locations that know the result of
C
include
\rho
and
anyone who already knew the output of
C
.
For example, the choreography
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.3\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}
reduces to
\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}.3
.

Choreographic conditionals, written
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}then}}~{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}else}}~{C_{2}}
, branch the entire choreography on the result of a local computation.
Here
C
must produce a boolean known to
\rho
.
Although the locations in
\rho
know which branch to take, other participants in the program may not.
This problem can be solved in two ways:
explicitly share the branch condition with all participants,
or include selection statements
{\ell}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}[}{d}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho^{\prime}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1};}}C
in the branches to inform locations in
\rho^{\prime}
of which branch was taken.
The former follows the literature on multiply-located values
(
Sweet et al. 2023
;
Bates et al. 2025
)
,
while the latter follows literature on selection messages
(
Montesi 2013
;
Hirsch and Garg 2022
;
Montesi 2023
;
Graversen et al. 2024
, see, e.g.,)
.
As the first choreographic language to incorporate both options,
\lambda
qc
allows added flexibility
and supports both of the following equivalent choreographies.

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}true}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}then}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.1}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}else}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.2}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}true}}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}then}}~{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}[}{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1};}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.1}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}else}}~{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}[}{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1};}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.2}\end{array}

The type abstraction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Lambda}{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\ldotp{C}
and type application
C~t
together implement polymorphism.
As described in Section
4.3
below,
\lambda
qc
has four kinds,
all of which are valid in type abstractions.

In addition,
\lambda
qc
includes typical algebraic and recursive data types including pairs, projections, injections,
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}case}}
expressions, and the isorecursive constructor
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}fold}}
and eliminator
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}unfold}}
.
These constructs have the standard semantics for a strict functional language
(
Pierce 2002
, Chapters 11 & 20)
, but note that they represent
global
data.
As a result, after evaluating a choreographic sum type, for instance,
all
participants know and agree on
whether the result is of the form
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}inl}}~{V_{1}}
or
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}inr}}~{V_{2}}
, although their knowledge of the contents of
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
may differ.

A major contribution of this work is the presence of
two

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}
expressions: one for local values and one for types.
Local-let expressions act like standard let expressions, binding local variables to the result of choreographic computations.
If
C_{1}
produces a value located at
\rho
, then
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\rho.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{C_{2}}

binds the result to variable
x
in the namespace of
\rho
, making it available in future local computations.
Importantly,
\rho
may be any subset of the locations who know the output of
C_{1}
.

Our new type-let expressions, written
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\rho.\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{C_{2}}
,
convert
representations
of locations, location sets, and types, into
actual
locations, location sets, and types.
They are semantically similar to a local-let binding above, except they bind type variables rather than local variables.
Specifically,
C_{1}
must produce a representation known to
\rho
of a location name, location set, or local type,
which is then reified to a type-level value and bound to
\alpha
in the body of
C_{2}
.
We elide
\rho
and
\kappa
for legibility when they are obvious from context.
Combining the type-let expression with the collecting sends described above allows arbitrary local language computations to dynamically select locations and propagate the choice to the choreographic level,
as shown in Example
3
.

Example 3 (Load Balancer).

Recall the example of a distributed thread pool from Section
1
.
Representations of type
\mathsf{loc}
are a value like any other,
so the local language could provide a function
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}tasks}:\mathsf{loc}\to\mathsf{int}
that returns the number of tasks assigned to a worker.
The (
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}
)anager can then balance the load between workers
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
by allocating computation
e
as follows:

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\alpha}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}.\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}if}~(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}tasks}({\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor})\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}<}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}tasks}({\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}))\\
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}then}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}else}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}\end{array}\right)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{\alpha.e\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}}

Say
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
currently has fewer tasks than
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
, so the local program will evaluate to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}
,
meaning the first expression in the let will evaluate to

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}.{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}\mathrel{\Longrightarrow_{c}}\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}.{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}

The type-let then reifies the representation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}
to an actual location, producing the step

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\alpha}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}.{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{\alpha.e\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}}\mathrel{\Longrightarrow_{c}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.e\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}

4.2.
\lambda
qc
Semantics

The operational semantics of
\lambda
qc
consists of a small-step relation using a labeled transition system of the form
C_{1}\mathrel{{\overset{R\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}_{c}}C_{2}
.
The label
R
represents a redex that tracks the specific reduction occurring.

Choreographies describe the actions of multiple locations,
and so distinct locations should be able to perform (unrelated) actions in any order.
To capture this idea, our semantics includes
out-of-order
reductions.
Importantly, operations for any one location should always execute in the specified order.
To enforce this requirement,
the semantics uses the redices in the step relation to determine which locations are involved in the step,
and only allows reordering of operations when the computation it is jumping before involves a disjoint set of locations.

\begin{array}[]{@{}llcl@{}}\textrm{Redices}&R&\Coloneqq&{\rho}.({e_{1}}\rightarrow{e_{2}})\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}App}}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu{\ell}.{m}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~\alpha.\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}t\end{array}

\small\begin{array}[]{l@{\qquad}l}\framebox{$\text{rloc}({R})$}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&\framebox{$\operatorname{cloc}({C})$}\\
\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{0.5em}}c@{\hspace{0.5em}}l}\text{rloc}({{\rho}.({e_{1}}\rightarrow{e_{2}})})\hskip 4.62497pt&=\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\rho\\
\text{rloc}({\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}App}}})\hskip 4.62497pt&=\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\mathcal{L}\\
\text{rloc}({{\ell}.{m}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}})\hskip 4.62497pt&=\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\{\ell\}\cup\rho\\
\text{rloc}({\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\rho.\alpha}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{t}})\hskip 4.62497pt&=\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\rho\end{array}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{0.5em}}c@{\hspace{0.5em}}l}\operatorname{cloc}({X})\hskip 4.62497pt&=\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\varnothing\\
\operatorname{cloc}({\rho.e})\hskip 4.62497pt&=\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\rho\\
\operatorname{cloc}({\mathop{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}fun}}}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{C}})\hskip 4.62497pt&=\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\varnothing\\
\operatorname{cloc}({C_{1}~C_{2}})\hskip 4.62497pt&=\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\mathcal{L}\\
\operatorname{cloc}({C\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.19376pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}}\rho})\hskip 4.62497pt&=\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\operatorname{cloc}({C})\cup\{\ell\}\cup\rho\\
\operatorname{cloc}({\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\rho.\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{C_{2}}})\hskip 4.62497pt&=\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\operatorname{cloc}({C_{1}})\cup\operatorname{cloc}({C_{2}})\cup\rho\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 3.

Selected Redices and Location Function Rules.
Here
m
is either a local value
v
or a selection label
d
.

More precisely, the
redex locations
function
\text{rloc}({R})
returns the set of locations involved in
R

and the
choreography locations
function
\operatorname{cloc}({C})
gives those involved in
C
.
For example, if
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
sends
v
to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
(denoted by the redex
{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}.{v}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}
),
then precisely
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
participate, so
\text{rloc}({{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}.{v}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}})=\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}
.
The
\operatorname{cloc}({C})
function operates on a whole choreography, not just a single step,
so even though
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
must take multiple steps before
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
gets involved,

\operatorname{cloc}({\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.1}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(1\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}x)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B})}})=\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}
.
Figure
3
shows selected redices and definitions of both location functions.
Note that all locations participate in choreographic function application, so
\operatorname{cloc}({C_{1}~C_{2}})=\text{rloc}({\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}App}}})=\mathcal{L}
.

It is then safe to move a step
R
before an entire computation
C
if the set of participants are disjoint (i.e.,
\operatorname{cloc}({C})\cap\text{rloc}({R})=\varnothing
),
even if a normal in-order execution would execute
C
before step
R
.
The following out-of-order rule for type-let expressions exhibits this structure,
also prohibiting the out-of-order step from including locations binding a variable in the let.

\small\frac{C_{2}\mathrel{{\overset{R\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}_{c}}C_{2}^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\operatorname{cloc}({C_{1}})\cap\text{rloc}({R})=\varnothing\hskip 9.24994pt\rho\cap\text{rloc}({R})=\varnothing}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\rho.\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{C_{2}}\mathrel{{\overset{R\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}_{c}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\rho.\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{C_{2}^{\prime}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left={\footnotesize[C-TyLetI]},vcenter}

\lambda
qc
also allows out-of-order execution in the branches of an if-expression before fully evaluating the branch condition.
Such a step is safe only when:
(1) the locations involved in the step are disjoint from those computing the branching condition, similarly to
C-TyLetI
(
??
),
and (2) this precise step is guaranteed to happen eventually.
The latter holds only if both branches take identical steps, which we enforce by requiring identical redices.
The result is the following
C-IfI
(
??
) rule.

\small\frac{C_{1}\mathrel{{\overset{R\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}_{c}}C_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994ptC_{2}\mathrel{{\overset{R\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}_{c}}C_{2}^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\operatorname{cloc}({C})\cap\text{rloc}({R})=\varnothing\hskip 9.24994pt\rho\cap\text{rloc}({R})=\varnothing}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}then}}~{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}else}}~{C_{2}}\mathrel{{\overset{R\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}_{c}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}then}}~{C_{1}^{\prime}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}else}}~{C_{2}^{\prime}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left={\footnotesize[C-IfI]},vcenter}

As an example, consider the choreography
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.e}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}then}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.(2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3)}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}else}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.(2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3)}
.
Although the condition is not yet evaluated,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
will run the same program on either branch, and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
is not involved in computing the branch condition.
That is, no matter what
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.e
evaluates to,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
will run
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.(2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3)
.
It is thus safe to reduce both branches to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.5
and the overall choreography to
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.e}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}then}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.5}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}else}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.5}
.
However, no reordering is possible in the following choreography.

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}~{\big(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.5\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A})}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(x\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}<}}4)}\big)}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}then}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.(2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3)}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}else}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.(2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3)}

Despite
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
performing identical computation in both branches and only
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
knowing which way the branch will go,
executing the branches before stepping
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.5\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
in the condition would reorder local operations for
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
, which is not permitted.

\frac{e_{1}\mathrel{\longrightarrow}e_{2}\quad\operatorname{fv}({\rho})=\varnothing}{\rho.e_{1}\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{{\rho}.({e_{1}}\rightarrow{e_{2}})}}{}_{c}}\rho.e_{2}}\quad\textsc{lab={\footnotesize[C-Done]},vcenter}\frac{\operatorname{Val}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}t{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}})\quad\operatorname{fv}({\rho})=\varnothing}{{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\rho.\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{\rho^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}t{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{C}\\
\hskip 25.00003pt\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\rho.\alpha}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{t}}}{}_{c}}\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto t\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto t]}{C[\alpha\mapsto t]}{C[\alpha\mapsto t]}\end{array}}}\quad\textsc{lab={\footnotesize[C-TyLetV]},vcenter}\frac{\operatorname{Val}({v})\quad L_{1}\in\rho_{1}\quad\operatorname{fv}({\rho_{2}})=\varnothing}{\rho_{1}.v\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}L_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}L_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}L_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.21529pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}L_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}}\rho_{2}\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{{L_{1}}.{v}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho_{2}}}}{}_{c}}(\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}).v}\quad\textsc{lab={\footnotesize[C-SendV]},vcenter}

Figure 4.
Selected
\lambda
qc
Operational Semantics

Figure
4
contains a selection of additional semantic rules.

C-Done
(
??
) shows how local programs execute in a choreography,

C-TyLetV
(
??
) shows how type representations are reified to types,
and
C-SendV
(
??
) formalizes the collecting message send semantics discussed in Section
4.1
.
Each rule also requires
\operatorname{fv}({\rho})=\varnothing
,
which simply demands all location variables to be resolved before taking a step.
Otherwise it would be impossible to know who is performing the computation.
Due to the out-of-order rules, this condition is nontrivial, even for closed choreographies.

The remaining rules are either very similar to those presented here or are standard for a strict functional language.
The complete semantics can be found in Appendix
A.7
.

4.2.1.
Substitution

The separation between choreographic variables, local variables, and type variables
results in three corresponding types of variable substitution.
Choreographic variable substitution, denoted
\mathchoice{C_{1}{\left[X\mapsto C_{2}\right]}}{C_{1}[X\mapsto C_{2}]}{C_{1}[X\mapsto C_{2}]}{C_{1}[X\mapsto C_{2}]}
, follows standard capture-avoiding substitution rules.
Local variable substitution for locations or location sets, denoted
\mathchoice{C{\left[\ell|x\mapsto e\right]}}{C[\ell|x\mapsto e]}{C[\ell|x\mapsto e]}{C[\ell|x\mapsto e]}
or
\mathchoice{C{\left[\rho|x\mapsto e\right]}}{C[\rho|x\mapsto e]}{C[\rho|x\mapsto e]}{C[\rho|x\mapsto e]}
, respectively, is similar,
but it operates only over the namespace of the location
\ell
(resp.
\rho
), which may itself be a type variable.
Notably, a multiply-located variable must be substituted for its entire namespace simultaneously,
so
\mathchoice{(\rho.x){\left[\rho|x\mapsto e\right]}}{(\rho.x)[\rho|x\mapsto e]}{(\rho.x)[\rho|x\mapsto e]}{(\rho.x)[\rho|x\mapsto e]}=\rho.e
, but
\mathchoice{(\{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\}.x){\left[\ell|x\mapsto e\right]}}{(\{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\}.x)[\ell|x\mapsto e]}{(\{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\}.x)[\ell|x\mapsto e]}{(\{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\}.x)[\ell|x\mapsto e]}
is undefined.

Type substitution, denoted
\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto t\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto t]}{C[\alpha\mapsto t]}{C[\alpha\mapsto t]}
, requires additional care when substituting locations or location sets.
A naïve implementation can capture variables due to namespace collisions.
For example, consider the following choreography with a location variable
\alpha
and concrete location
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}
.

\begin{array}[]{l}C=\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\alpha.x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}\alpha.2\\
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}.x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}.3\\
\alpha.y\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}.x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\alpha\\
\end{array}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~\alpha.(x\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}y)\end{array}\end{array}

Because
\alpha
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}
are (syntactically) distinct, the local variables
\alpha.x
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}.x
are also distinct,
so this choreography should always produce
\alpha.5
regardless of the concrete location to which
\alpha
resolves.

But when
\alpha
resolves to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}
, how do we define
\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}]}{C[\alpha\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}]}{C[\alpha\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}]}
?
One might think that, since nothing binds
\alpha
in
C
, we can simply replace all instances of
\alpha
with
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}
.
However, doing so makes the previously-distinct local variables
\alpha.x
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}.x
collapse.
The variable
\alpha.x
is incorrectly captured by the definition of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}.x
and the choreography would wrongly evaluate to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}.6
.
Note that, when substituting
\mathchoice{{\left[\alpha\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}\right]}}{[\alpha\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}]}{[\alpha\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}]}{[\alpha\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}]}
, capture can occur when binding either
\alpha.x
or
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}.x
.
Inside a binding of
\alpha.x
, free instances of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}.x
in the body will be captured,
and so too will free instances of
\alpha.x
be captured when substituting under a binding of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}.x
.

To avoid this namespace capture, substitutions of locations in local-let expressions must rename variables when binding within
either
namespace.
The following rules define
safe location set substitution for local-let bindings, with single location substitution defined as a special case by replacing the set
\sigma
with the singleton
\{\ell\}

in the side conditions.
Here
\operatorname{fv}_{\rho}({C})
denotes the local variables free in
C
for
any
location in
\rho
.

\small\mathchoice{(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\rho.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{C_{2}}){\left[\alpha\mapsto\sigma\right]}}{(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\rho.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{C_{2}})[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\rho.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{C_{2}})[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\rho.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{C_{2}})[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[t]{@{}r@{~}l@{}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}&{(\mathchoice{\rho{\left[\alpha\mapsto\sigma\right]}}{\rho[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{\rho[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{\rho[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}).x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{\mathchoice{C_{1}{\left[\alpha\mapsto\sigma\right]}}{C_{1}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{C_{1}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{C_{1}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}&{\mathchoice{C_{2}{\left[\alpha\mapsto\sigma\right]}}{C_{2}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{C_{2}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{C_{2}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}}\end{array}&\text{if}~\alpha\subseteq\rho~\text{and}~x\notin\operatorname{fv}_{\sigma-\rho}({C_{2}})\\[18.49988pt]
\begin{array}[t]{@{}r@{~}l@{}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}&{(\mathchoice{\rho{\left[\alpha\mapsto\sigma\right]}}{\rho[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{\rho[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{\rho[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}).y}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{\mathchoice{C_{1}{\left[\alpha\mapsto\sigma\right]}}{C_{1}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{C_{1}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{C_{1}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}&{\mathchoice{\mathchoice{C_{2}{\left[\rho|x\mapsto y\right]}}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}{\left[\alpha\mapsto\sigma\right]}}{\mathchoice{C_{2}{\left[\rho|x\mapsto y\right]}}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{\mathchoice{C_{2}{\left[\rho|x\mapsto y\right]}}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{\mathchoice{C_{2}{\left[\rho|x\mapsto y\right]}}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\text{if}~\alpha\subseteq\rho,x\in\operatorname{fv}_{\sigma-\rho}({C_{2}}),\\
\text{and}~y\notin\operatorname{fv}_{\rho\cup\sigma}({C_{2}})\end{array}\\[18.49988pt]
\begin{array}[t]{@{}r@{~}l@{}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}&{\rho.y}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{\mathchoice{C_{1}{\left[\alpha\mapsto\sigma\right]}}{C_{1}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{C_{1}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{C_{1}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}&{\mathchoice{\mathchoice{C_{2}{\left[\rho|x\mapsto y\right]}}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}{\left[\alpha\mapsto\sigma\right]}}{\mathchoice{C_{2}{\left[\rho|x\mapsto y\right]}}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{\mathchoice{C_{2}{\left[\rho|x\mapsto y\right]}}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{\mathchoice{C_{2}{\left[\rho|x\mapsto y\right]}}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}{C_{2}[\rho|x\mapsto y]}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\text{if}~\alpha\nsubseteq\rho,\sigma\cap\rho\neq\varnothing,\\
x\in\operatorname{fv}_{\alpha}({C_{2}}),\text{and}~y\notin\operatorname{fv}_{\rho\cup\alpha}({C_{2}})\end{array}\\[18.49988pt]
\begin{array}[t]{@{}r@{~}l@{}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}&{\rho.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{\mathchoice{C_{1}{\left[\alpha\mapsto\sigma\right]}}{C_{1}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{C_{1}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{C_{1}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}&{\mathchoice{C_{2}{\left[\alpha\mapsto\sigma\right]}}{C_{2}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{C_{2}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}{C_{2}[\alpha\mapsto\sigma]}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\text{if}~\alpha\nsubseteq\rho~\text{and}~\text{either}\\
\sigma\cap\rho=\varnothing~\text{or}~x\notin\operatorname{fv}_{\alpha}({C_{2}})\end{array}\end{cases}

4.3.
\lambda
qc
Kinding System

We now turn to our static semantics, which has two components: an elementary kinding system, and a typing system.
For notational brevity we assume throughout that new variables are always fresh.
A kinding judgment takes the form
\Gamma\vdash t::\kappa
, where
\Gamma
is a kinding context,
t
is a type, and
\kappa
is a kind.
The kind
\kappa
classifies
t
as either a program type (
*
), a location (
*_{\ell}
), a set of locations (
*_{s}
), or a local program type (
*_{e}
).
Figure
5
presents the syntax for the
\lambda
qc
types and kinds.

\begin{array}[]{@{}llcl@{}}\textrm{Kinds}&\kappa&\Coloneqq&*\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu*_{\ell}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu*_{s}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu*_{e}\par\\
\textrm{Local Program Types}&t_{e}&\Coloneqq&\alpha\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{bool}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{tyRep}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{loc}_{\rho}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{locset}_{\rho}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\ldots\par\\
\textrm{Locations}&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\ldots&\in&\mathcal{L}\par\\
\textrm{Choreography Types}&\tau,\ell,\rho,t&\Coloneqq&\alpha\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mut_{e}@\rho\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\tau_{1}\rightarrow\tau_{2}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\forall\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa.\tau\\
&&\mkern 3.0mu\mid&\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mu\alpha.\tau\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\{\ell\}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}\end{array}

Figure 5.
Syntax of Types and Kinds. Here
\alpha
is a type variable.

All program types of kind
*
