directly extend their analogues from System F, except for base types.
Our base types take the form
t_{e}@\rho
and represent a single local program of type
t_{e}
running at all locations in the set
\rho
.
The kind
*_{\ell}
represents location names, which can refer to either concrete locations
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}\in\mathcal{L}
, or in-context location variables.
The kind
*_{s}
classifies (non-empty) finite sets of location names, which can be either a type variable, a singleton set (
\{\ell\}
), or a union of sets (
\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}
).
Finally, types of kind
*_{e}
are precisely the types included in the local language under a given type variable context.
Notably, local types may use type variables bound in the choreography, even if the local type system does not include a type variable binding mechanism.

The full kinding rules can be found in Appendix
B.1
.

4.4.
\lambda
qc
Type System

The second component of our static semantics is the type system.
Typing judgments for
\lambda
qc
take the form
\Gamma;\Delta;\Sigma\vdash C:\tau
,
where
\Gamma
is a kinding context, as described above,

\Delta
is a choreographic typing context that handles variables bound by choreographic functions and case expressions,
and
\Sigma
is a local typing context.
These contexts are handled in a standard manner for a polymorphic type system
(
Pierce 2002
, Chapter 23)
.
The local typing context
\Sigma
is a list of ascriptions of the form
\rho.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{e}

tracking variables bound by local let expressions (see Section
4.1
).
Figure
6
contains a selection of typing rules for
\lambda
qc
.

\frac{\Gamma\vdash\rho::*_{s}\quad\Gamma;\left.\Sigma\right|_{\rho}\Vdash e:t_{e}\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash\Delta\quad\Gamma\vdash\Sigma}{\Gamma;\Delta;\Sigma\vdash\rho.e:t_{e}@\rho}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[T-Done]},vcenter}\frac{\Gamma;\Delta;\Sigma\vdash C:t_{e}@\rho_{1}\quad\quad\ell\in\rho_{1}\quad\Gamma\vdash\rho_{2}::*_{s}}{\Gamma;\Delta;\Sigma\vdash C\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.21529pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}}\rho_{2}:t_{e}@(\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2})}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[T-Send]},vcenter}\frac{\Gamma;\Delta;\Sigma\vdash C_{1}:t_{e}@\rho_{2}\quad\rho_{1}\subseteq\rho_{2}\quad\quad\Gamma;\Delta;\Sigma,\rho_{1}.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{e}\vdash C_{2}:\tau}{\Gamma;\Delta;\Sigma\vdash\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\rho_{1}.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{e}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{C_{2}}:\tau}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[T-LetLocal]},vcenter}\frac{\Gamma;\Delta;\Sigma\vdash C_{1}:\mathsf{loc}_{\rho_{1}}@\rho_{3}\quad\rho_{1}\subseteq\rho_{2}\subseteq\rho_{3}\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash\tau::*\quad\Gamma,\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\ell};\Delta;\Sigma\vdash C_{2}:\tau}{\Gamma;\Delta;\Sigma\vdash\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\rho_{2}.\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\ell}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{C_{2}}:\tau}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[T-LetLoc]},vcenter}

Figure 6.
Selected Typing Rules

The
T-Done
(
??
) rule type-checks local computations by appealing to the local type system, but it needs to know which local variables are in scope.
In a multiply-located computation
\rho.e
, every location in
\rho
should compute the same result,
so each free variable in
e
must be bound with the same meaning at every location in
\rho
.
To check this requirement, we define a projection operation
\left.\Sigma\right|_{\rho}
that restricts
\Sigma

to only those local variables bound in a namespace
\rho^{\prime}
where
\rho\subseteq\rho^{\prime}
.
Formally,

\left.\Sigma\right|_{\rho}=\begin{cases}\cdot&\text{if}~\Sigma=\cdot\\
\left.\Sigma^{\prime}\right|_{\rho},x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{e}&\text{if}~\Sigma=\Sigma^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{e}~\text{and}~\rho\subseteq\rho^{\prime}\\
\left.\Sigma^{\prime}\right|_{\rho}&\text{if}~\Sigma=\Sigma^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{e}~\text{and}~\rho\not\subseteq\rho^{\prime}\end{cases}

The
T-Send
(
??
) rule ensures (1) that only local values can be sent by requiring
C
to have type
t_{e}@\rho_{1}
,
and (2) that the sender (
\ell
) must know the value being sent.
By locating the output type at
\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}
, it captures the multiply-located collecting semantics described in Section
4.2
.

The other two rules in Figure
6
are the two forms of let binding.
The
T-LetLocal
(
??
) rule for local-let bindings is very similar to a traditional let binding rule.
It just restricts to binding local values—
C_{1}
has type
t_{e}@\rho_{2}
—and requires that

\rho_{1}
(the locations binding
x
) must all have access to the result of the computation
C_{1}
—that is,
\rho_{1}\subseteq\rho_{2}
.

The
T-LetLoc
(
??
) rule for type-let expressions, core to supporting first-class location names, is much more subtle.
Indeed, a naïve definition could introduce type dependency or unsoundness.
Type dependency is the simpler concern, and is addressed by the premise
\Gamma\vdash\tau::*
,
which demands that
\tau
be a well-formed type in the context
\Gamma

without the newly-bound variable
\alpha
.
As a result, while the body
C_{2}
of the let may freely reference
\alpha
, its
type

\tau
may not.
This restriction prevents the type of the entire let expression from depending on the value of
C_{1}
, thereby avoiding value dependency in the type system.
As an example, the program below on the left is not well-typed, because the body has type
\mathsf{int}@\alpha
in which
\alpha
is free.
By contrast, the program on the right is well-typed because the body, and the choreography itself, has the type
\mathsf{int}@\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
.

{}\nvdash\left(\begin{array}[]{r@{~}l}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}&{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\alpha}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}&{\alpha.(1\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}1)}\end{array}\right):\tau{}\vdash\left(\begin{array}[]{r@{~}l}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}&\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}.\alpha\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}.{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\rfloor}\\
&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}\alpha.(1\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}1)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.x\end{array}\right):\mathsf{int}@\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}

The other unusual premise to
T-LetLoc
(
??
) is
\rho_{1}\subseteq\rho_{2}\subseteq\rho_{3}
, which is necessary to ensure soundness.
To understand why, recall Example
3
, in which the thread-pool manager
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}
dynamically assigns a task to either
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
or
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
.
Consider what happens if
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}
were to inform only
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
and the client
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
of their decision, but not
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
, the selected worker.
As
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
is not aware that she should compute
e
and send back the result,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
will wait forever on a message from
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
that never arrives, causing a deadlock.

Amazingly, the simple premise
\rho_{1}\subseteq\rho_{2}\subseteq\rho_{3}
prevents all deadlocks resulting from the type-let expression.
As described in Section
3.1
,
\rho_{1}
is a static upper bound on the set of values to which the dynamically generated location may resolve.
The location variable is bound in the namespace of
\rho_{2}
, so only those locations may use the result,
and all locations in
\rho_{3}
know the value of the dynamically generated location name.
The subset relationship therefore ensures that any location to which
\alpha
may resolve will bind
\alpha
,
and that all locations that bind
\alpha
know the value.
In the unsound hypothetical above,
\rho_{1}=\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}
and
\rho_{2}=\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}
, violating this premise.

The full type system, which can be found in Appendix
B.2
,
is sound with respect to the operational semantics, as demonstrated by the following mechanically verified theorems.

Theorem 1

(Type Preservation)
.

If
\Gamma;\Delta;\Sigma\vdash C_{1}:\tau
and
C_{1}\mathrel{\Longrightarrow_{c}}C_{2}
, then
\Gamma;\Delta;\Sigma\vdash C_{2}:\tau
.

Theorem 2

(Progress)
.

If
\,\vdash C_{1}:\tau
, then either
C_{1}
is a value, or there is some
C_{2}
such that
C_{1}\mathrel{\Longrightarrow_{c}}C_{2}
.

5.
Network Language

Choreographies specify the behavior of concurrent systems,
and compiling them to a set of programs that can actually run concurrently at different locations requires a language to specify that system.
We thus provide a
network language
for
\lambda
qc
.
It specifies programs at individual locations and how to compose them into a parallel system with concurrent operational semantics.

5.1.
Network Language Syntax

\begin{array}[]{@{}llcl@{}}\textrm{Network Program}&E&\Coloneqq&X\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({e})\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}send}}~{E}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}to}}~{\rho}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}recv}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}from}}~{\ell}\\
&&\mkern 3.0mu\mid&E_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}E_{2}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathop{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fun}}}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0muE_{1}~E_{2}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\Lambda}{\alpha}\ldotp{E}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0muE~t\\
&&\mkern 3.0mu\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E_{1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{E_{2}}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E_{1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{E_{2}}\\
&&\mkern 3.0mu\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}~(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}})~(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}\Rightarrow{E_{2}})\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choose}}~{d}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}for}}~{\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}{E}\\
&&\mkern 3.0mu\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}if}}~{E}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{E_{1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{E_{2}}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\in}}~{\rho}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{E_{1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{E_{2}}\\
\textrm{Systems}&\Pi&\Coloneqq&L_{1}\triangleright E_{1}\mathrel{\parallel}\ldots\mathrel{\parallel}L_{n}\triangleright E_{n}\\
\end{array}

Figure 7.
Selected Network Program Syntax. Here
L\in\mathcal{L}
is a concrete location name.

The network language is a concurrent
\lambda
-calculus where messages are values from the same local language as choreographies.
The syntax is similar to the choreographic syntax from Section
4
, except sending and receiving messages are now split into two separate constructs.
Figure
7
contains selected syntactic forms, using the same variable naming conventions as choreographies.
The full definition is available in Appendix
C.1
.

The network language counterpart of the choreographic local computation
\rho.e

is the return expression
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({e})
, which similarly executes the local language program
e
.
Since a network program is only for a single location
L
, it should only run
e
when
L\in\rho
and do nothing otherwise.
We include a unit value
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}
to represent a network program that does nothing.

Choreographies have a single message sending operation, which represents both sending and receiving the message.
As the network language describes only a single location’s behavior, it splits these into two constructs:

\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}send}}~{E}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}to}}~{\rho}
which multicasts the results of
E
to every location in
\rho
,
and
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}recv}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}from}}~{\ell}
, which receives a local value from location
\ell
.
Note that
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}send}}
can only send local values, so
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}send}}~{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}to}}~{\rho}
, for instance, would be stuck.

Both local-let and type-let expressions are mirrored identically from choreographies to the network language,
as are recursive functions, type abstractions, and applications for both.
The network language also includes a primitive sequencing operator
E_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}E_{2}

for a similar reason to why it includes
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}
.
Specifically, a location
L
may have computation to perform in the head and body of a choreographic let expression,
yet not be included in the locations binding a variable.
In this case,
L
will sequence their actions from the two parts of the let expression.

Finally, the network language contains three branching mechanisms for different purposes:
the standard
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}if}}~{E}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{E_{1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{E_{2}}
construct,

\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow-choice}}}
to implement choreographic selection statements,
and
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\in}}
, which branches based on the identity of the current location.

Recall from Section
4.1
that a selection statement
{\ell}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}[}{d}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1};}}C

has
\ell
send
d
to each location in
\rho
so they know which branch to take in a choreographic
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}
statement.
As with message sends, the network language splits the construct in two: sending and receiving.
Each recipient is waiting on an
external choice
.
Some other location
\ell
must pick either
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}
or
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}
, represented by the expression
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}~(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}})~(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}\Rightarrow{E_{2}})
,
which will execute
E_{1}
after receiving
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}
or
E_{2}
after receiving
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}
.
Meanwhile,
\ell
knows the choice
d
and can send it to all locations in
\rho
before executing
E
using the expression
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choose}}~{d}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}for}}~{\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}{E}
.

Note that an
\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow-choice}}}
with only one branch, such as
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}~({\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}}\Rightarrow{E})
, is valid.
This one-sided branch will execute
E
if
\ell
sends
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}
, but will get stuck if
\ell
sends
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}
.
We write
E_{\bot}
for the program in a branch that might be missing.

The last form of branching is the “AmI-In” expression
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\in}}~{\rho}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{E_{1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{E_{2}}
, which conditions on whether the currently executing location is in
\rho
.
That is, when executing at location
L
,
E_{1}
will execute if
L\in\rho
, and
E_{2}
will execute otherwise.
This construct generalizes the “AmI” expression of PolyChor
\lambda

(
Graversen et al. 2024
)
,
which uses a single location
\ell
instead of a set
\rho
, and branches based on equality with
\ell
rather than inclusion.
We write
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{E_{1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{E_{2}}
as a shorthand for
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\in}}~{\{\ell\}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{E_{1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{E_{2}}
.

5.2.
Network Language Operational Semantics

\begin{array}[]{@{}llcl@{}}\textrm{Transition Labels}&l&\Coloneqq&\iota\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\iota_{\text{sync}}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu{m}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu{L}.{m}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}\end{array}

\frac{e_{1}\mathrel{\longrightarrow}e_{2}}{L\triangleright\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({e_{1}})\mathrel{{\overset{\iota\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({e_{2}})}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[N-Ret]},vcenter}\frac{f=\mathop{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fun}}}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E}\quad\operatorname{Val}({V})}{L\triangleright f~V\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{\iota_{\text{sync}}}}{}}\mathchoice{E{\left[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V\right]}}{E[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}{E[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}{E[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[N-App]},vcenter}\frac{\operatorname{Val}({v})\quad\operatorname{fv}({\rho})=\varnothing}{L\triangleright\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}send}}~{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({v})}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}to}}~{\rho}\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{{v}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho\setminus\{L\}}}}{}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({v})}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[N-Send]},vcenter}\frac{\operatorname{Val}({v})\quad L^{\prime}\neq L}{L\triangleright\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}recv}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}from}}~{L^{\prime}}\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{{L^{\prime}}.{v}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}}}{}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({v})}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[N-Recv]},vcenter}\frac{\operatorname{fv}({\rho})=\varnothing}{L\triangleright\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choose}}~{d}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}for}}~{\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}{E}\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{{d}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho\setminus\{L\}}}}{}}E}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[N-Choose]},vcenter}\frac{L^{\prime}\neq L}{L\triangleright\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{L^{\prime}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\\
\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}}\\
\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}\Rightarrow{{E_{2}}_{\bot}}\end{array}\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{{L^{\prime}}.{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}}}{}}E_{1}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[N-AllowL]},vcenter}

Figure 8.
Selected Network Language Operational Semantics

The network language semantics is a labeled transition system
L\triangleright E_{1}\mathrel{{\overset{\smash{\scriptstyle l}\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}}E_{2}

where
L
is the location executing the program and
l
is the label on the step.
Just as network programs specify the operations of a single location, the labels only acknowledge the view of that location.
Figure
8
shows selected transition labels and rules,
with the full semantics available in Appendix
C.3
.

The
\iota
label denotes an internal step,
where either the network program or the local program reduces without interaction between locations.
The
N-Ret
(
??
) rule for local steps shows an example use of
\iota
.

The
synchronized
internal label
\iota_{\text{sync}}
denotes internal steps where
all
locations must take that step at the same time.
It is used for steps like
\beta
-reduction (
N-App
(
??
)) where
the corresponding choreographic step modifies the choreography for all participants.
Synchronization in these cases is a technical requirement needed to prove correctness of the system,
which we compare to other approaches in Section
8.1
.

The labels
{m}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}
and
{L}.{m}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}
appear with message sends and receives, respectively, including selection messages.
As recipients do not know the value in advance,
N-Recv
(
??
) is non-deterministic, allowing any value to arrive.
The system semantics below ensures senders and receivers agree on the message.
The same is true for
N-AllowL
(
??
) and a symmetric
N-AllowR
rule.
The sender’s perspective is governed by
N-Send
(
??
) and
N-Choose
(
??
), which ensure that the transition label matches the message and recipients intended by the program.

5.2.1.
Network Systems

\begin{array}[]{@{}llcl@{}}\textrm{System Label}&l_{S}&\Coloneqq&\iota\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0mu\iota_{\text{sync}}\mkern 3.0mu\mid\mkern 3.0muL_{1}.m\rightsquigarrow\rho\end{array}

\frac{L\triangleright\Pi(L)\mathrel{{\overset{\iota\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}}E}{\Pi\mathrel{{\overset{\iota\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}_{S}}\mathchoice{\Pi{\left[L\mapsto E\right]}}{\Pi[L\mapsto E]}{\Pi[L\mapsto E]}{\Pi[L\mapsto E]}}\quad\textsc{lab={\footnotesize[Internal]},vcenter}\frac{\forall L\in\text{dom}(\Pi)\ldotp\Big(L\triangleright\Pi(L)\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{\iota_{\text{sync}}}}{}}\Pi^{\prime}(L)\Big)}{\Pi\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{\iota_{\text{sync}}}}{}_{S}}\Pi^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab={\footnotesize[Sync-Internal]},vcenter}\frac{L_{1}\notin\rho\quad L_{1}\triangleright\Pi(L_{1})\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{{m}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}}}{}}E_{1}\quad\quad\forall L\in\rho\ldotp\Big(L\triangleright\Pi(L)\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{{L_{1}}.{m}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}}}{}}E_{L}\Big)\quad}{\Pi\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{L_{1}.m\rightsquigarrow\rho}}{}_{S}}\mathchoice{\Pi{\left[L_{1}\mapsto E_{1},\mkern 2.0mu\rho\mapsto E_{L}\right]}}{\Pi[L_{1}\mapsto E_{1},\mkern 2.0mu\rho\mapsto E_{L}]}{\Pi[L_{1}\mapsto E_{1},\mkern 2.0mu\rho\mapsto E_{L}]}{\Pi[L_{1}\mapsto E_{1},\mkern 2.0mu\rho\mapsto E_{L}]}}\quad\textsc{lab={\footnotesize[Comm]},vcenter}

Figure 9.
System Semantics and Labels

A choreography specifies
interactions
between multiple locations,
which we represent with a parallel composition of network programs at different locations.
Formally, a
system

\Pi={}{\parallel_{L\in\mathfrak{L}}(L\triangleright E_{L})}

maps each location
L
in a finite set
\mathfrak{L}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
to the network program
E_{L}
it is currently executing.
The system semantics is itself a larger labeled transition system, with combined labels shown in Figure
9
.
While network program labels show only one side of a send or receive, system labels reflect both sides
and the semantics ensures that senders and recipients agree on the content of a message.

Figure
9
also shows how to lift the operational semantics of the network language to systems with three rules.

Internal
(
??
) allows one location to independently take an internal step.

Sync-Internal
(
??
) allows all locations to simultaneously perform a synchronized step.

Comm
(
??
) parties sending and receiving messages together,
requiring the sender and
all
specified recipients step at the same time with the same message value.
Notationally,
\mathchoice{\Pi{\left[\rho\mapsto E_{L}\right]}}{\Pi[\rho\mapsto E_{L}]}{\Pi[\rho\mapsto E_{L}]}{\Pi[\rho\mapsto E_{L}]}
denotes the updated system mapping
L
to
E_{L}
if
L\in\rho
and
\Pi(L)
otherwise.

6.
Endpoint Projection

With the compilation target fixed, we turn to the endpoint projection procedure
that defines how to compile a choreography into a concurrent network system.

6.1.
Network Program Merging

It is important to keep the projection definition
compositional
for simplicity and scalability,
but choreographic
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}
statements complicate this process and necessitate a
merge operator
.
To understand why, consider the following choreography
C
.
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.e~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}then}}
({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}[}{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1};}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.1)
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}else}}
({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}[}{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1};}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.2)
\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\\
\mid{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}}\Rightarrow{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({1})}\end{array}
\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\\
\mid{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}}\Rightarrow{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({2})}\end{array}
\sqcup
=
\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\\
\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}\Rightarrow{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({1})}\\
\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}\Rightarrow{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({2})}\end{array}
=
C
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}

In the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}then}}
branch,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
will always send
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
the selection message
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}
.
In the projection of this branch,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
should therefore wait to receive
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}
and then return
1
.
There are no instructions for what to do if
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
receives
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}
in this branch—which will never happen—so that side of the
\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow-choice}}}
is empty.
Similarly, in the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}else}}
branch,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
can only ever receive
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}
, so the
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}
side of the
\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow-choice}}}
is empty.

\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\\
\mid{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}}\end{array}\right)\sqcup\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\\
\mid{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}}\Rightarrow{E_{2}}\end{array}\right)=\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\\
\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}}\\
\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}\Rightarrow{E_{2}}\end{array}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\\
\mid{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}}\end{array}\right)\sqcup\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\\
\mid{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}^{\prime}}\end{array}\right)=\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\\
\mid{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}\sqcup E_{1}^{\prime}}\end{array}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\\
\mid{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}}\end{array}\right)\sqcup\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\\
\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}^{\prime}}\\
\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}\Rightarrow{E_{2}}\end{array}\right)=\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\\
\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}\sqcup E_{1}^{\prime}}\\
\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}\Rightarrow{E_{2}}\end{array}

Figure 10.
Selected Merge Operator Definitions

In the full
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}
statement, however, both branches are possible, so
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
’s projection must include both options.
To compositionally combine the
\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow-choice}}}
statements from each branch, we use a
merge
operator
E_{1}\sqcup E_{2}
.
Specifically,
\sqcup
is an idempotent partial binary function defined structurally homomorphically on matching network programs
that incorporates
\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow-choice}}}
branches that exist on only one side (and homomorphically merges those that exist in both).
Figure
10
shows a few of the rules defining merge, with the full definition available in Appendix
D.1
.

This merge operator is extremely similar to the one in Pirouette
(
Hirsch and Garg 2022
)
,
but we allow functions to merge if their bodies merge, not just if their bodies are syntactically equal.

6.2.
Endpoint Projection Definition

Using this merge function, we can define endpoint projection (EPP).
The projection of a choreography
C
to a location (endpoint)
L
, denoted
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}
, is the network program
L
runs to implement its part of
C
.
EPP is partial (i.e., a choreography may fail to project) both because the merge operator is partial and because it must verify that locations only use type variables which they have bound.

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\rho.e\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\rho.e\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\rho.e\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\rho.e\rrbracket_{L}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({e})&\text{if}~L\in\rho\\
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.19376pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}}\rho\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.19376pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}}\rho\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.19376pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}}\rho\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.19376pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}}\rho\rrbracket_{L}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}send}}~{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}to}}~{\rho}&\text{if}~L=\ell\\
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}\mathrel{\fatsemi}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}recv}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}from}}~{\ell}&\text{if}~L\neq\ell~\text{and}~L\in\rho\\
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left\llbracket{\ell}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}[}{d}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1};}}C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket{\ell}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}[}{d}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1};}}C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket{\ell}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}[}{d}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1};}}C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket{\ell}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}[}{d}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1};}}C\rrbracket_{L}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choose}}~{d}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}for}}~{\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}}&\text{if}~L=\ell\\
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}~({d}\Rightarrow{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}})&\text{if}~L\neq\ell~\text{and}~L\in\rho\\
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}then}}~{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}else}}~{C_{2}}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}then}}~{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}else}}~{C_{2}}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}then}}~{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}else}}~{C_{2}}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}then}}~{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}else}}~{C_{2}}\rrbracket_{L}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}if}}~{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}}&\text{if}~L\in\rho\\
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}\mathrel{\fatsemi}(\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}\sqcup\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}})&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Lambda}{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\ldotp{C}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Lambda}{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\ldotp{C}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Lambda}{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\ldotp{C}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Lambda}{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\ldotp{C}\rrbracket_{L}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\Lambda}{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\ell}}\ldotp{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}~{\alpha}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto L\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto L\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto L\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto L\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}\rrbracket_{L}}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}}}&\text{if}~\kappa=*_{\ell}\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\Lambda}{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{s}}\ldotp{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\in}}~{\alpha}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}\rrbracket_{L}}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}}}&\text{if}~\kappa=*_{s}\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\Lambda}{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\ldotp{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\rho.\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\ell}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{C_{2}}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\rho.\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\ell}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{C_{2}}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\rho.\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\ell}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{C_{2}}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\rho.\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\ell}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{C_{2}}\rrbracket_{L}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[t]{@{}r@{~}l@{}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}&{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\ell}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}}\\
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}&{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}~{\alpha}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\mathchoice{C_{2}{\left[\alpha\mapsto L\right]}}{C_{2}[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C_{2}[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C_{2}[\alpha\mapsto L]}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C_{2}{\left[\alpha\mapsto L\right]}}{C_{2}[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C_{2}[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C_{2}[\alpha\mapsto L]}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C_{2}{\left[\alpha\mapsto L\right]}}{C_{2}[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C_{2}[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C_{2}[\alpha\mapsto L]}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C_{2}{\left[\alpha\mapsto L\right]}}{C_{2}[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C_{2}[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C_{2}[\alpha\mapsto L]}\rrbracket_{L}}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}}}\end{array}&\text{if}~L\in\rho\\
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}\mathrel{\fatsemi}\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}&\text{if}~L\notin\rho~\text{and}~\alpha\notin\operatorname{fv}({\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}})\\
\text{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Figure 11.
Selected EPP Definitions

EPP is defined as a structurally recursive function over the syntax of the choreography.
Most of the rules simply convert choreographic syntax into its network language counterpart,
but in some cases more involved translation is required.
Figure
11
shows these cases.

The first four projection definitions capture intuitions described previously.
For
\rho.e
, only locations in
\rho
should compute
e
while others should do nothing.
For
C\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.21529pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}}\rho
, everyone should perform their portion of the computation specified by
C
,
then location
\ell
should send a message, locations in
\rho
should receive a message, and that is all.
For selection messages, location
\ell
should announce the choice, while other locations in
\rho
wait for it
and condition their behavior on the result.
As there is only one choice here, the
\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow-choice}}}
only has one branch.
The other branch can appear when projecting conditionals,
where everyone first performs any computation specified by the condition,
then locations who know the result of that condition—locations in
\rho
—simply branch,
while EPP combines the branches for other locations using the merge operation defined above.
Merging can fail, so EPP also fails for this location if it is undefined.

Sequencing Function

Note that the rules do not directly use the network language’s native sequencing primitive
E_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}E_{2}
,
but instead use a
collapsing sequencing function

E_{1}\mathrel{\fatsemi}E_{2}
defined by

E_{1}\mathrel{\fatsemi}E_{2}=\begin{cases}E_{2}&\text{if}~\operatorname{Val}({E_{1}})\\
E_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}E_{2}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

This can be seen as a peephole optimization to eliminate null sequences
whose primary purpose is to enable projected systems to properly simulate out-of-order choreographic steps.
For instance, without collapsing sequenced values,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
’s projection of the choreography

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.e}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.(2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3)}
would be
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3})
.
An out-of-order step at
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
would reduce to
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.e}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.5}
,
but to simulate this step, the projection would need to reduce to
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({5})
,
which is impossible because the reduction occurs to the right of a semicolon.
The actual EPP defined above, however, projects these choreographies to
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({2\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}3})
and
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({5})
, respectively, eliminating the concern.

Collapsing values also adds flexibility to EPP.
To see why, consider the following choreography.

C=\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.e}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}then}}~{(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(1+1)}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.3})}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}else}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.3}

Intuitively,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
takes identical actions—returning
3
—in both branches, so
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}
should simply be
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({3})
.
However, without the sequencing function, the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}then}}
branch would project to
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({3})
, while the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}else}}
branch would project to
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({3})
.
These network programs do not merge, so
C
would not project.
Collapsing the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}then}}
branch to
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({3})
allows the expected projection.

Process Polymorphism

The final two projections in Figure
11
concern location (set) abstraction.
Choreographic type abstractions project to network type abstractions.
When the type variable is a location (
*_{\ell}
) or location set (
*_{s}
), however,
the body must behave differently depending on how the location executing relates to the value the type variable resolves to.

Graversen et al. 2024
solve this problem with the
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}
construct,
executing
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto L\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto L\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto L\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto L\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}\rrbracket_{L}}
when the locations match and
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}
when they do not.
When the locations match, this projection is clearly correct.
When they do not, the projection is correct because EPP treats location variables as abstract identifiers that are equal only to themselves,
meaning
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}
will behave as though
\alpha\neq L
.

Location
set
abstraction generalizes this idea.
Replacing
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}
with
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\in}}
is straightforward, as is
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}
treating
\alpha
as an abstract set where
L\notin\alpha
.
The other branch, where
\alpha
resolves to a set containing
L
, is more subtle.
This projection of
C
must treat
\alpha
as though it contains
L
,
without changing the meaning of
\alpha
.
We accomplish this by explicitly adding
L
to the set in this branch and using
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}{C[\alpha\mapsto\{L\}\cup\alpha]}\rrbracket_{L}}
.
The symbolic containment relation recognizes that
L\in\{L\}\cup\rho
for any
\rho
, including the variable
\alpha
,
so the projection will properly treat
L
as being in the set.
Moreover, despite symbolically changing the set, the operational semantics remain correct.
This branch only executes when
\alpha
resolves to some
\rho
where
L\in\rho
, in which case
\{L\}\cup\rho=\rho
.

Finally, type-let expressions combine a structurally recursive projection, also used in local-let expressions, with the location-based conditionals of type abstraction.
For locations binding the type variable, the projection first computes the type, binds it, and then uses
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}
to condition based on its value.
For other locations, EPP simply sequences the head of the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}
with its body.
Notably, the type system, to avoid dependency, does not prevent locations outside of
\rho
from referencing
\alpha
.
Any such invalid reference would leave
\alpha
unbound in the body of the let, so EPP checks that
\alpha\notin\operatorname{fv}({\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}})
.
If both
\alpha
is free and
L\notin\rho
, there is no correct way for
L
to execute its part of
C_{2}
, so the projection is undefined.
The projection for type-let expressions where
\alpha
has kind
*_{s}
are nearly identical,
with
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\in}}
in place of
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}
and the approach described above for type abstractions when
L\in\rho
.

This EPP definition gives the network program corresponding to one location.
Combining these programs into a parallel system (see Section
5
) gives an executable interpretation of the entire choreography.
Specifically, we lift EPP point-wise to a finite set of locations
\mathfrak{L}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
, defining
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}={\parallel_{L\in\mathfrak{L}}(L\triangleright\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}})}
,
which also requires
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}
to be defined for all
L\in\mathfrak{L}
.

6.3.
Bisimulation Relation

Next, we will examine the relationship between our choreographic operational semantics and the semantics given by EPP, with two main goals in mind.
Firstly, to exhibit a bisimulation between these two semantics to justify the correctness of EPP.
Secondly, to give a guarantee of deadlock freedom by design for compiled systems.

To construct a bisimulation, we must choose which systems are related to a given choreography.
The natural choice is to say that a choreography
C
is related only to its projection
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}
.
However, this relation is too strict.
When a choreography branches, the branch which is not taken is discarded,
but in the projected system, that branch may be preserved by locations waiting on selection messages.
For instance, consider the following example where
C_{1}
reduces to
C_{2}
.
\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}true}}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}then}}~{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}[}{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1};}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.1}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}else}}~{{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}[}{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1};}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.2}\end{array}\end{array}

{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}[}{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1};}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.1
\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\\
\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}\Rightarrow{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({1})}\\
\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}\Rightarrow{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({2})}\end{array}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\\
\mid{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}}\Rightarrow{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({1})}\end{array}\end{array}

C_{1}=
=C_{2}
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}=
=\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}
c
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}if}}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}true}
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}
\neq

Once
C_{1}
takes the left branch,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
can never send the selection message
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}
to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
.
While this makes sense from
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
’s perspective, it does not make sense for
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
, whose projected program has discarded the
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}
branch without receiving any input from
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
.
We expect that choreographic steps will only affect the projection of locations involved in the step.

An additional wrinkle stems from EPP’s use of the collapsing sequencing function
E_{1}\mathrel{\fatsemi}E_{2}
:
local programs (in an arbitrary subexpression) which resolve to a value after a substitution may be removed from the projected program, as in the following example.
\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\begin{array}[t]{@{}r@{~}l@{}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}&{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}.1}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}&{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathop{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}fun}}}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}\\
{\kern 5.0pt\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.y}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}.x}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.2}}\end{array}}\end{array}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathop{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}fun}}}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}\\
{\kern 5.0pt\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.y}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}.1}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.2}}\end{array}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({1})}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}\\
\mathop{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fun}}}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({x})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}}\end{array}
\mathop{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fun}}}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}}
C_{1}=
=C_{2}
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}=
=\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}
c
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{1}
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}
\diagup

This reduction substitutes
1
for
x
in the body of the function.
While
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}
can analogously substitute
x
, the resultant program
\mathop{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fun}}}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({1})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}}
differs from
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}
,
as the projection collapses the body of the function to
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({1})\mathrel{\fatsemi}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}=\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}
.
As the mismatch is in the function body, there are no steps
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
can take to correct it.

We account for both types of mismatches using a relation
E_{1}\preceq E_{2}

indicating that
E_{1}
may have discarded some unneeded code—either choices or sequenced values—that
E_{2}
retains.
Formally, it is the smallest partial order on network programs that is structurally compatible—it admits rules like

\frac{E_{1}\mkern 3.0mu\preceq\mkern 3.0muE_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447ptE_{2}\mkern 3.0mu\preceq\mkern 3.0muE_{2}^{\prime}}{E_{1}\mkern 2.0mu\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}\mkern 2.0muE_{2}\mkern 3.0mu\preceq\mkern 3.0muE_{1}^{\prime}\mkern 2.0mu\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}\mkern 2.0muE_{2}^{\prime}}
—and admits the following three rules.
The first two handle additional branches, while the third covers collapsing sequences.

\frac{E_{1}\preceq E_{1}^{\prime}}{\begin{array}[]{@{\mkern 2mu}l@{\mkern 2mu}}.0mu\lx@intercol\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\hfil\mkern 2.0mu\\
.0mu\lx@intercol\mid{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}}\hfil\mkern 2.0mu\end{array}\preceq\begin{array}[]{@{\mkern 2mu}l@{\mkern 2mu}}.0mu\lx@intercol\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\hfil\mkern 2.0mu\\
.0mu\lx@intercol\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}^{\prime}}\hfil\mkern 2.0mu\\
.0mu\lx@intercol\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}\Rightarrow{E_{2}^{\prime}}\hfil\mkern 2.0mu\end{array}}\frac{E_{2}\preceq E_{2}^{\prime}}{\begin{array}[]{@{\mkern 2mu}l@{\mkern 2mu}}.0mu\lx@intercol\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\hfil\mkern 2.0mu\\
.0mu\lx@intercol\mid{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}}\Rightarrow{E_{2}}\hfil\mkern 2.0mu\end{array}\preceq\begin{array}[]{@{\mkern 2mu}l@{\mkern 2mu}}.0mu\lx@intercol\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\hfil\mkern 2.0mu\\
.0mu\lx@intercol\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}L}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}^{\prime}}\hfil\mkern 2.0mu\\
.0mu\lx@intercol\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.67,0.33,0.1}R}}\Rightarrow{E_{2}^{\prime}}\hfil\mkern 2.0mu\end{array}}\frac{E_{1}\preceq E_{2}\quad\operatorname{Val}({V})}{E_{1}\preceq V\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}E_{2}}

The relation also lifts point-wise to systems (with identical domains
\mathfrak{L}
):

\Pi_{1}\preceq\Pi_{2}~=~\forall L\in\mathfrak{L}\ldotp\Pi_{1}(L)\preceq\Pi_{2}(L).

By relaxing the bisimulation to relate a choreography
C
not only to
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}
,
but to any system
\Pi
where
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}\preceq\Pi
, we avoid the problems described above.
In the first example,
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}\preceq\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}
,
and in the second,
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}\mathrel{\Longrightarrow}E
where
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}\preceq E
, as needed.

6.4.
Soundness, Completeness, and Deadlock Freedom

The above correspondence is sufficient to show that our choreographic and projected system semantics are equivalent and yield deadlock freedom of compiled systems as a result.
To this end, we prove the choreographic semantics is sound and complete with respect to the projected system.

The theorems require that all
location names in
C
, denoted
\operatorname{LN}({C})
,
are included in the projected system:
\operatorname{LN}({C})\subseteq\mathfrak{L}
.
This requirement formalizes the implicit assumption that all locations specified by the choreography are present in the system,
lest, for instance,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
be unable to compute
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.2\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
because
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
is not in the system.
We similarly require
\mathfrak{L}\neq\varnothing
to ensure
someone
is computing.

The completeness theorem is straightforward, and says the projected semantics simulate the choreographic semantics.

Theorem 3

(Completeness)
.

If
\Gamma;\Delta;\Sigma\vdash C:\tau
,
C\mathrel{\Longrightarrow^{n}_{c}}C^{\prime}
, and
\operatorname{LN}({C})\subseteq\mathfrak{L}\neq\varnothing
,
then there is some
\Pi
and
k\geq n
such that
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}\mathrel{\Longrightarrow^{k}_{S}}\Pi
and
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}\preceq\Pi
.

The mechanized proof first shows the system can simulate a single step and then extends it to multiple steps by induction.
It also uses the fact that, if
E_{1}\preceq E_{2}
and
E_{1}
is the projection of a choreography,
then
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
can make the same reductions, plus
E_{2}
possibly taking administrative steps to remove extra un-collapsed semicolons.

We would like a soundness theorem that says the choreographic semantics can simulate the projected system,
but there are some technical complications.
First, after a single reduction in a projected system, the reduced system does not necessarily correspond to any choreography, and may instead be some intermediate state.
The following example shows a case where the projected system
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}}
can reduce to a state
\Pi
which no choreography projects to.
\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\triangleright\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({2+3})\\
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\triangleright\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({2+3})\end{array}
\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}.(2+3)
\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\triangleright\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({5})\\
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\triangleright\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({2+3})\end{array}
=
\Pi
\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\triangleright\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({5})\\
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\triangleright\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({5})\end{array}
\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}.5
C_{1}=
=C_{2}
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}}=
=\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}}
c
S
S
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}}
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}}

To reach the system corresponding to the projection of
C_{2}
,
\Pi
must continue to progress.
As a result, even our single-step soundness theorem must allow the system to continue to reduce
until it reaches a point where the original choreography can match its progress.

The second technicality is a fundamental challenge stemming from the combination of nontermination and multiply-located local computations.
In the system, each participant in a multiply-located local computation evaluates it independently.
If one location performs such a step while another is blocked by an earlier infinite loop, however, the choreography will be unable to match the system.
For instance, in
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}let}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\coloneqq}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}loop}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}in}}~{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}.(1\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}1)}
the only possible choreographic step is to run the infinite
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}loop}
at
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
,
but in the projected system,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
may reduce the local expression
1\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}+}}1
to
2
.

To avoid such situations, our soundness theorem only holds for choreographies
C
where all local computations terminate.
Specifically, if
C\mathrel{\Longrightarrow^{*}_{c}}C^{\prime}
and
\rho.e
is in an evaluation position of
C^{\prime}
, then
e
must terminate.
Importantly, this condition does not require the local
language
to be terminating, only the specific local computations that execute in
C
.

With these two caveats in place, we can now state a single-step version of soundness.

Proposition 1

(Single-Step Soundness)
.

If
\,\vdash C:\tau
,
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}\mathrel{\Longrightarrow_{S}}\Pi
,
\operatorname{LN}({C})\subseteq\mathfrak{L}\neq\varnothing
,
and all local programs that
C
executes terminate, then for some
\Pi^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime}
,
\Pi\mathrel{\Longrightarrow^{*}_{S}}\Pi^{\prime}
,
C\mathrel{\Longrightarrow^{+}_{c}}C^{\prime}
, and
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}}\preceq\Pi^{\prime}
.

Extending this result to simulate multiple system steps with a naïve induction runs into another problem:
the steps that we wish to simulate and the extra—possibly different—steps needed to ensure the system is not in an intermediate state
are independent reduction sequences. To reduce the endpoints of these diverging paths to a common system, we employ a confluence theorem, which critically relies on confluence of the local language.

Proposition 2

(System Confluence)
.

If
\Pi_{1}\mathrel{\Longrightarrow^{*}_{S}}\Pi_{2}
and
\Pi_{1}\mathrel{\Longrightarrow^{*}_{S}}\Pi_{3}
